# Preface.

---

There is a type of moral mysticism <note>The author uses the term "sittlicher Mysticismus" to describe a school of thought that relies on spiritual intuition rather than rational logic to determine right and wrong.</note> which insists that all the principles by which we must guide our actions be derived from a supposed supernatural experience. According to this view, the morality of an action is to be demonstrated through the revelation of the consequences that follow it, while the voice of reason is to be entirely excluded from the process. The origin of this way of thinking is as venerable as its consequences are harmful. It arises from men of the best intentions—individuals who feel deeply and vividly that human beings are elevated far above mere sensory pleasure, but who lack the intellectual cultivation and strength of spirit to find the ground <note>The text breaks off here at the end of the page. The next page likely continues the thought regarding the "ground" or "foundation" of morality.</note>

<summary>In this opening to the preface, the author introduces and critiques "moral mysticism," a philosophy that replaces rational ethics with supposed supernatural revelations. While acknowledging the noble intentions of its followers, the author warns that their lack of intellectual rigor leads to harmful consequences.</summary>

<keywords>moral mysticism, ethics, reason, revelation, sensory pleasure, principles of action, German Enlightenment philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>This page continues the sentence from page 3, which ended with the word "ground." The author is completing the thought that well-meaning but uncritical individuals fail to find the rational basis for their moral feelings, leading them toward mysticism. The catchword at the bottom "digt" completes the word "kündigt" (announces/heralds) on the following page.</meta>

<image-desc>A horizontal typographic ornament consisting of symmetrical flourishes and scrolls.</image-desc>

of this feeling within their reason, they commonly cling to it. It is harmful in its consequences because it hinders the use of reason, works against the development of the noblest human dispositions, and abandons the most sublime moral concepts to the distortions of a hidden, though often coarse, sensuality <note>In this philosophical context, "sensuality" (Sinnlichkeit) refers to the part of human nature governed by physical senses and instincts rather than rational or moral principles.</note>, and to the deceptions of the most cunning fraudsters who understand how to play and execute the role of an emissary of the deity.

I am not speaking here of this kind of mysticism, but rather of one that is far worse; one which, while originating from the former, immediately moves in almost the opposite direction. Moral mysticism is based on the noble part of the human being—even if it operates unrecognized—and bears witness to a kind-hearted and powerful disposition. It does not reject the concepts of right and wrong in the least, and errs only through the imbalance in the cultivation of its theoretical and practical reason <note>These are core terms in Enlightenment philosophy: "theoretical reason" deals with what we can know, while "practical reason" deals with how we ought to act.</note>, caused by its failure to recognize the true origin of these concepts. It does indeed preach virtue, and fails only in determining its motives <term>Triebfedern: literally "drive-springs," the internal mechanisms or incentives that move a person to act.</term>. However, the mysticism of which I shall speak in the following sections announ-

<summary>The author distinguishes between "moral mysticism," which is well-intentioned but intellectually flawed, and a more dangerous form of mysticism that leads to the manipulation of others and the subversion of reason. He argues that moral mysticism fails not in its goals, but in its understanding of the rational foundations of virtue.</summary>

<keywords>reason, sensuality, moral mysticism, virtue, theoretical reason, practical reason, motives of action, Enlightenment philosophy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues from page 4, completing the word "kündigt" (proclaims/announces). The author is contrasting a "better" form of mysticism with a "worse" one that demands total submission to a secret hierarchy.</meta>

proclaims open war against all virtue, regards it as a matter of **policing** <note>In the 18th century, "Polizey" (policing) referred not just to a police force, but to the general administrative regulation of public behavior and order.</note>, and denies it both internal dignity and actual value. He places the destiny of man, the ultimate purpose of all humanity, not in the **realization of moral maxims**, but in a **blind obedience to God**. However, since this God can neither deal with us in a sensory way—at least not until we are purified in and through the Order—nor can He be recognized in His laws through **reason**, but instead connects with us only through the high superiors of the **Order of the Knights of the Faith** <note>This likely refers to a specific secret society or a branch of the Rosicrucians or "Strikte Observanz" Freemasonry, which were common targets of Enlightenment critics.</note>, since they alone are His organ through which He reaches us and we reach Him, then blind obedience to God means nothing other than a blind obedience to the superiors of the Order. 

This blind obedience then also demands the recognition of everything that the Order presents as truth and high wisdom. He who honestly strives to follow the good he has recognized, to clarify his moral concepts and investigate their source, and who leaves no faculty within himself uncultivated, is to him a rebel against the **deity** and guilty of eternal death. The villain, however, as soon as he renounces his own investigation and his own rea-

<summary>This page criticizes a dangerous form of religious mysticism that replaces individual moral reason with absolute, blind obedience to the leaders of a secret "Order." It argues that such systems strip virtue of its inherent value by making it a matter of external control rather than internal conviction.</summary>
<keywords>blind obedience, Order of the Knights of the Faith, moral maxims, religious authoritarianism, Enlightenment vs. Mysticism, virtue as policing</keywords>

use of **reason** <note>original: "nunftgebrauch", completing "Vernunftgebrauch" from the previous page.</note>, as soon as he blindly carries out everything that an unknown **superior of the Order** <term>Ordensoberer: The high-ranking leaders of secret societies like the Rosicrucians or the Strict Observance Freemasons, whose identities were often kept secret from lower-ranking members.</term> has ordered him to do through a third party as a command of the **Deity**, he is, in the eyes of this Order, a saint. Murderers, thieves, and **mystics** of this class therefore have this in common: that the concept of **property** <note>The author is comparing the mystic's rejection of intellectual ownership (reason) to a criminal's rejection of physical ownership.</note> with all its derivatives is utterly hateful to them as soon as it is applied to an object outside themselves, and they cannot even bear to hear the word by which it is designated. The former extend their hatred to the physical, while the latter extend it to the spiritual.

Only to the completely inactive man, who conducts himself merely passively—who moves only when and in the direction he is pushed—only to this contemptible creature is it possible, according to him, to reach the destiny of man. Only such a person can develop that so highly praised faculty within himself, through which he now hears what has not yet entered any mortal's ear; sees what no human eye has yet beheld; understands what surpasses all human **reason**; and feels what was previously quite foreign to the human heart <note>This passage parodies 1 Corinthians 2:9 ("Eye has not seen, nor ear heard..."), suggesting that these mystics claim a divine revelation that bypasses the human mind.</note>. Through this faculty, he is meant to become a **demigod**, penetrate the secret meaning of the **Bible**, and stand in full light, while we others

To graft such a **mysticism** onto the **Protestant religion** is now the most zealous endeavor of a society of a few men in — **America** <note>In the late 18th century, European writers often used "America" as a setting for allegories or to describe the spread of radical new ideas, though it may also refer to specific transatlantic missionary or occult activities.</note> — at whose head stands a man animated by the spirit of **Hermes Trismegistus** <term>Hermes Trismegistus: A legendary Hellenistic figure perceived as the author of the Hermetica, a body of texts providing the basis for Hermeticism, which blends philosophy, magic, and alchemy.</term>, but whom we have preferred to call the **holy Balthasar**. The Saint! — — because, according to the assurances of his followers, he will surely one day perform miracles, and thus perhaps be of more use through his **skull** <note>A sarcastic jab at the veneration of relics; the author suggests the leader's dead remains will be more valuable than his living thoughts.</note> than he was able to be during his entire life with his living head. Balthasar! — because it pleases him quite extraordinarily to play the role of a **spiritual king** and to rule over priests in the manner of worldly regents. The author initially wanted to bestow upon him the epithet **wise**, but he withdrew it because this adjective — as he remembered just in time — must not be profaned, but may only be used within the secret **circles of the Order**.

Who told you then, dear countryman,

1) that my story rests merely on **personalities** and could not one day also be performed in **Germany**? — Believe it, dear countryman, it will indeed be played among us as well; **mysticism** will certainly venture the voyage across the sea and haunt <note>The German "heimsuchen" implies both a visit and an affliction, suggesting the author views this spread of ideas as a looming threat.</note> us **Germans** too, in order to complete what the **war**, unfortunately! has begun.

2) My story is entirely understandable, even then, should it remain an insoluble riddle who is actually meant by the **holy Balthasar**. We **Germans**, admittedly, do not have the good fortune of knowing the **American Saint** face to face, but we have nevertheless become acquainted with his spirit through this story; and what does the **mask** <note>original: "Larve", a term used here to describe a deceptive facade or a persona that hides one's true nature.</note> concern us, as soon as we are able to look through it and know what [lies] behind it

<meta>This page continues from the previous page, completing the sentence regarding the "mask" of Balthasar and why the author is publishing original documents from a secret order.</meta>

-><image-desc>A decorative horizontal printer's ornament consisting of symmetrical flourishes and a central oval element.</image-desc><-

is hidden from her? In order not to misjudge **Balthasar’s** spirit, and not to miss a single trait that characterizes him, I have had the records printed which are kept in the archive of the **Knights of the Faith** <note>original: "Ritter des Glaubens"; a fictional or satirical secret society modeled after contemporary mystical or Masonic orders.</note>, which explain the first degree of the **Order** and flowed from his own pen. Therefore, before I say a word, before I dare a single brushstroke to characterize him, the **Saint** presents himself before my readers and performs the first act of that spectacle to which, without me, access would have been barred to most of us.

As soon as ignoble principles animate a **secret society**, as soon as men of intellect stand at its head who only ever cast as much darkness upon their **initiates** <term>Eingeweihten: members who have been formally admitted into the secret knowledge of a group</term> as they can digest; as soon as they go to work methodically to corrupt both head and heart, there is much to be feared from such a society. However, the entire phantom vanishes as soon as it ceases to creep in the dark and can be scrutinized <note>The original "beaugstigt" is likely an archaic variant of "beäugt," meaning to be eyed or closely inspected.</note> in broad daylight.

This is the service I have wished to render to the world.

## of Saint Balthasar.
<note>While "Saint Balthasar" traditionally refers to one of the three Biblical Magi, in this context it likely refers to a legendary founder or a high-ranking pseudonym within the secret society's mythology.</note>

**How** do the Catechumen Brothers <note>A "catechumen" traditionally refers to a person receiving basic instruction in Christianity before baptism. In secret societies, it was commonly used to designate the lowest rank or novices.</note> make themselves known through their coughing, in order to be admitted into our assemblies?

As do the Knights of the Faith in general. <note>This implies a shared secret sign or "touch" used by all members regardless of rank, though the specific nature of the cough is not detailed here.</note>

All Knights of the Faith together have but a single final purpose — the general final purpose of the Order. The Catechumene <note>This sentence ends abruptly as the text continues on the following page.</note>

<summary>This page serves as the title and opening of a ritualistic catechism for the Order of the Knights of the Faith, attributed to a figure named Saint Balthasar. It introduces the concept of secret signs, specifically coughing, used by initiates to gain entry to meetings.</summary>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues the sentence from page 11. The catchword "ne" from the previous page completes the word "Catechumene" (Catechumen) at the beginning of this text. The catchword "bens" at the bottom of this page suggests the next page will begin with the conclusion of a word ending in "-bens" (likely "Lebens," meaning "of life").</meta>

[Catechume]ne <note>The text begins mid-word, completing "Catechumene" from the previous page.</note> strives thereafter to obtain the same thing that has been bestowed upon the Chosen; it is therefore also absolutely necessary that he makes use of the same means. All grades, through which we must pass in order to reach the innermost Sanctuary, <note>Original: "Heiligthum." In the context of 18th-century secret societies, this refers to the highest or most secret degree, often metaphorically linked to the Holy of Holies in the Temple of Solomon.</note> are absolutely necessary parts of the Order, for the perfect whole consists of all these parts; if even a single one of them were missing, it would be an imperfect body.

Each of these parts is equal to the whole—it is a perfect outline of the whole in miniature. Since, therefore, the lower divisions, or the individual parts of the Order, are similar to the whole in every respect, we must also—in order to be admitted into the assemblies of the lower divisions, to complete this degree, and to receive the reward of the Catechumens—make use of the same means in our weakness, or according to the proportion of our strength, through which the perfected Knight of the Faith <note>A "Knight of the Faith" (Ritter des Glaubens) refers to a member who has reached a high level of initiation within this specific society.</note>

<term>Chosen</term> (Auserwählten) — Those who have reached the higher mysteries; <term>Sanctuary</term> (Heiligthum) — The inner core or highest degree of the Order; <term>Order</term> (Orden) — The secret society as a whole; <term>Catechumens</term> (Katechumenen) — The lowest rank or novices; <term>Knight of the Faith</term> (Ritter des Glaubens) — A title for an advanced member.

<summary>This page outlines the organizational philosophy of the Order, arguing that the secret society is a "perfect body" where every rank, from novice to master, is essential and mirrors the whole. It explains that initiates must use the same spiritual or ritual means at the beginning of their journey as those used by perfected members at the end.</summary>
<keywords>Order of the Knights of the Faith, Catechumen, hierarchy, Sanctuary, secret society degrees, Enlightenment philosophy</keywords>

[o]ne <note>original: "ne"; likely completing "Einer" (Someone/One) from the previous page.</note> strives to obtain the selfsame thing that has been granted to the Chosen <note>The "Chosen" or "Elect" (Auserwählten) refers to those who have attained the highest spiritual insights or highest ranks within the society.</note>; it is therefore absolutely necessary that he makes use of the selfsame means. All degrees <term>degrees</term> (Grade) — the sequential ranks or levels of initiation within the Order. that we must pass through in order to reach the innermost sanctuary <term>sanctuary</term> (Heiligthum) — the symbolic "Holy of Holies" or the ultimate internal truth of the society. are absolutely necessary parts of the Order, for the perfect whole consists of all these parts; if even a single one of them were missing, it would be an imperfect body.

Each of these parts is equal to the whole—it is a perfect blueprint of the whole in miniature. Since the lower divisions, or the individual parts of the Order, are similar to the whole in every respect, we must also—in order to be admitted into the meetings of the lower divisions, to complete this degree, and to receive the reward of the catechumens <term>catechumens</term> (Katechumenen) — a term traditionally used for those receiving basic Christian instruction, here adopted to mean entry-level initiates of the Order.—make use of the same means (in our weakness or in proportion to our strengths) through which the perfected Knight of the Fai- <note>The text cuts off mid-word; the catchword provided indicates the word completes as "Faith" (Glaubens).</note>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page completes the word "Glaubens" (Faith) from the previous page's final sentence and continues the ritualistic question-and-answer session (catechism) regarding the symbolic actions of the Order's initiates.</meta>

-th <note>original: "bens"; completing "Glaubens" (Faith) from the previous page.</note> with the intention of the whole, have known how to gain entrance. We Knight of Faith catechumens <term>catechumens</term> (Katechumenen) — entry-level initiates who are still being instructed in the basic principles of the Order. thus cough in the selfsame manner as the Knights of the Faith in general.

And why do you merely cough, and not knock, as is common in the profane world <term>profane world</term> (profane Welt) — a term used by secret societies to describe the secular or uninitiated world outside their fellowship. when one wishes to gain entrance to a room?

To indicate that faith has not yet triumphed, but must hide itself from this wicked and evil world and withdraw into solitude.

But why do you cough exactly three times, and the third time more strongly than the first two?

Partly for the sake of the Trinity <term>Trinity</term> (Dreyeinigkeit) — the Christian doctrine of God as three persons (Father, Son, and Holy Spirit) in one essence., and partly because of the three punishments which God presented to David to atone for his error when he had the Israelite people counted! <note>This refers to the biblical narrative in 2 Samuel 24, where King David is offered a choice of three punishments for his prideful census: famine, defeat in war, or pestilence. He chooses the third, considering it better to fall into the hands of God than man.</note> I coughed more strongly the third time for the sake of the third and harshest punishment.

<summary>This page explains the symbolic meaning of coughing instead of knocking as a ritual sign of entry, connecting the practice to the soul's need for solitude and the biblical story of King David's punishments.</summary>
<keywords>catechism, ritual coughing, profane world, Trinity, King David, initiation, symbolism</keywords>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page continues the explanation of the ritualistic cough mentioned on the previous page, transitioning from the symbolic number of coughs to the moral lesson regarding the sin of pride, as exemplified by King David.</meta>

<image-desc>A decorative horizontal printer's ornament featuring symmetrical floral and scrollwork elements.</image-desc>

We therefore cough <note>The OCR reads "hasten" (to hurry), but the context of the previous page and the ritual catechism confirms this is "husten" (to cough).</note> three times as a constant and emphatic warning to carefully guard ourselves against the root of all vices—against that first evil through which angels and humans fell. This is, namely, nothing other than haughtiness or pride. # To seek the Light.

How old are you, my Brother?

Three months and some days over.

Why do you answer thus?

Because I have climbed the first three steps to the door of the Temple and have worked upon the lowest.

We, as **Catechumens** <term>Catechumens: Historically, those receiving basic instruction in Christianity before baptism. Here, it refers to the entry-level members or "novices" of the Order who are being prepared for deeper mysteries.</term>, are "three months and some days over," specifically in consideration of how we have climbed the first three steps to the stairs of the **Temple** and have worked on the lowest—that is, in the near vicinity of the Temple, of the Holy and the Most Holy <note>The author refers to the layout of the Temple of Solomon, which was divided into the Porch, the Holy Place, and the Holy of Holies. The initiate's work begins on the outside.</note>. In this regard, a certain age is already attributed to us—the age of the **renewed man** <term>Renewed Man: A concept from Christian mysticism (particularly Pietism) referring to the spiritual rebirth of a person who has turned away from worldly vanity to seek divine truth.</term>—but as we see, it is a very weak and childish age in which we cannot yet exist at all without beneficial, guardian-like attendance and care. In the absence of such care, we would necessarily perish and die in this so tender age. As three-

As infants only months old, we already recognize (and in this consists our entire knowledge) our mother's breast; we then learn to distinguish our Father from other beings of his kind, and after a while, we begin to stammer his name—though still very imperfectly and poorly. In many mystical traditions, particularly those influenced by Pietism or certain Catholic devotions, the "Sacred Heart" or "Bleeding Heart" of Jesus represents the core of divine love and the source of spiritual nourishment.</note> We know what children at this age eventually learn about their father: that he, the Lord our Father, is our sole provider and benefactor. Why is it not asked: Have you received your earned reward, or even simply, your reward? In such a case, we would be able to answer, or give back the second letter. **Why does one begin by seeking to frighten you before admitting you into the Order?**

To ensure my perseverance and to see whether I possess all the necessary qualities so that the true faith can take root within me.

Such caution is highly necessary and a very wise regulation of the Order. <note>The "Order" likely refers to a fraternal or esoteric organization, such as the Freemasons or Rosicrucians, which utilized elaborate initiation rituals to test a candidate's character.</note> Nothing is more common than a quick, enthusiastic resolution. <meta>This page continues the catechism or ritual explanation started on the previous page, shifting from the general requirements of the Order to the specific symbolism of the initiation ritual, specifically the stripping of worldly garments and the indirect path taken to enter the assembly.</meta>

<page-num>28</page-num>

<image-desc>A decorative horizontal vignette or printer's ornament consisting of symmetrical scrolls and floral motifs.</image-desc>

What is the meaning of the removal of your clothes?

The first happy age of the world, or the **golden age**, in which that hideous idol of the current century—**Reason**—had not yet developed to its presumptuous height; a time when man required neither clothes nor shining metals, and thus these things were not yet able to lead his heart astray. <note>The "Golden Age" refers to a mythical state of primordial innocence. Here, "Reason" is criticized in the context of the 18th-century Enlightenment; the Order suggests that intellectual pride and material wealth have corrupted humanity's original spiritual purity.</note>

Why were you led into our assembly through indirect paths?

Partly to signify that the believer, alas! can only enter if he—enduring shame for **Christ's** sake—wishes to be certain of reaching his holy ultimate purpose; and partly to make me realize what difficulties one must first overcome before finding the path of faith and virtue.

This is a wise and charitable precaution of the Order. It tells and shows us in advance what we, in our endeavor— <note>The text breaks off here, as indicated by the catchword "ben" (likely part of "begegnen" or "befinden") at the bottom of the original page.</note>

All these three knocks <note>original: *Schläge*; refers here both to the physical ritual knocks and the metaphorical "blows" or steps of spiritual progression.</note> we must have actually struck—these rules and regulations truly passed through—and the promises <term>Verheißungen; divine assurances found in scripture</term> placed upon them truly experienced; then, after the third and final hard knock, which we must perform with special emphasis, entrance will be granted to us. --- Page 92 ---
<meta>This page continues the sentence from page 79, completing the thought regarding the Pridonians' path toward humanity and the psychological hurdles they faced.</meta>

humanity, yet such was nonetheless the case. Their primary sickness of the soul <note>original: "Seelenkrankheit"; a term common in 18th-century psychology and philosophy to describe a mental or spiritual affliction, often used by Enlightenment thinkers to describe "primitive" states of mind</note> consisted of superstition, which had mastered their imagination, placed their understanding <term>Verstand: in Enlightenment philosophy, the faculty of logic and empirical concepts</term> in shameful shackles, mutilated their moral feeling, and suppressed their reason <term>Vernunft: the higher faculty of universal principles and moral truths</term>. Everything to them was the effect of higher powers, without whom nothing could be accomplished, and who stood, at most, only in close connection with supernatural humans. What incentive would there have been for them to develop their own powers and to think of their own activity—which to them was the most superfluous thing in the world? 

The sphere of what they could comprehend was extremely small. Consequently, they could not even sense the laws of nature in their full extent; at most, they could dimly suspect some connection between what preceded an action and what followed it, provided that the action did not exceed the sphere of the ordinary. They were far less able to find the intermediate links through which the incomprehensible connects to the recognized order of nature. To them, the Europeans provided with firearms <note>original: "Feuergewehr"; likely referring to muskets or early rifles which appeared "magical" to the uninitiated</note> were demigods,

--- Page 93 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of the Europeans whom the Pridonians initially perceived as demigods, completing the thought from page 79 regarding the transformative effect of technology on their worldview.</meta>

who had stolen lightning and thunder from heaven or had been voluntarily equipped with them by the same, and who walked here below in human form only for the sake of holding judgment in the name of the gods and cultivating justice. It was as if scales fell from their eyes <note>original: "Wie Schuppen fiel es daher von ihren Augen"; a biblical reference to the restoration of sight, symbolizing a sudden realization or the shedding of a delusion</note> and the most violent revolution occurred in their heads when they learned to produce saltpeter, to extract sulfur, to manufacture gunpowder, to bring rifles to completion, and with them to bring down the bird from the air in its swiftest flight. 

With the belief in one of the most eminent effects of invisible powers and its incomprehensibility, that belief collapsed entirely; superstition vanished, the feeling of the nobility of their nature <note>original: "Gefühl der Hoheit ihrer Natur"; a classic Enlightenment concept referring to the realization of human dignity and the potential of the human mind over "primitive" fear</note> was stirred within them, a spirit of inquiry <term>Forschungsgeist: the intellectual drive to investigate and understand the natural world through empirical evidence rather than myth</term> began to form, and their entire attention was fixed on the Germans, their teachers. The German language became all the more widespread as the number of artists and craftsmen increased and several things emerged from non-existence <note>meaning these objects or concepts were newly introduced to their culture</note> for which there were only German names.

<summary>This page describes the "demythologization" of the Europeans in the eyes of the Pridonians, as the latter learn the chemistry and mechanics behind firearms, leading to the collapse of superstition and the adoption of the German language and scientific inquiry.</summary>
<keywords>Enlightenment, gunpowder, firearms, superstition, spirit of inquiry, German language, technological transfer, cultural transformation</keywords>

After these preparations, and after such a prior processing of their unrefined minds <note>original: "rohen Geistes"; referring to a state of nature before "civilized" education</note>, the theologians only then set their hands to the work of their conversion.

Just as Darando <note>likely a leading figure or legislator in the narrative's fictional or historical context</note> had striven to enliven the powers of the Pridonians and to give them such a direction that they resulted in the harmony of the well-being of the whole, so too did they strive to develop the morally good among them, to maintain it, to increase it, and thereby give these newly developed powers the proper direction, to ennoble their hearts, to familiarize their reason with the highest ultimate purpose of humanity, and in this way to enlighten them expediently concerning the supersensible <term>Uebersinnlichen: a philosophical term for things beyond the reach of the physical senses, such as God or the soul</term> and the future after death. This path, which they followed in their work of conversion, had the most desirable consequences.

<meta>This page continues the critique of the religious faction (led by Balthasar and Geisterfreund) that has gained influence in the empire. With them, openness, frankness <term>Freymüthigkeit: a key Enlightenment value involving the courage to speak one’s truth openly and honestly</term>, nobility of spirit, and the spirit of inquiry <note>original: *Forschungsgeist*</note> gradually began to decline—at least among certain classes of the people—and in their place stepped secretiveness, hypocrisy, malice, and blind faith. Bit by bit, people forgot how they had raised themselves to a position of power and brought the Consistory <term>Konsistorium: the administrative council responsible for church government and clerical discipline</term> under their complete dominion. In place of the accustomed love for God and virtue there arose a depraved obsession with pleasing them, and with selling the truth for the sake of profitable offices. 

Ignorance aligned itself with them, and baseness followed in their train. It became a crime to preach virtue or to teach the Christian religion according to the spirit of Jesus. The babbling of incomprehensible formulas became the fashion, and common sense <note>original: *gesunde Menschenverstand*</note> seemed to have abandoned the pulpits. The vicious were protected in their offices, while the virtuous were sought to be ousted; they preferred, as they expressed it, to receive "pure wine from impure hands, rather than impure wine from the purest hands." <note>This metaphor suggests the faction preferred orthodox dogma from corrupt men over "tainted" or liberal theology from morally upright men.</note> Only the most virtuous

Balthasar was a proud enthusiast <term>Schwärmer: a derogatory term used during the Enlightenment to describe religious fanatics or "visionaries" who were perceived as having abandoned reason for irrational zeal</term>, puffed up by his own knowledge and filled with rage against anyone who thought differently. To be sure, gentle words flowed from his lips and honey from his mouth; he boasted of being so tolerant that he did not even hate the devil, but only pitied him, claiming he wished only to ensure the maintenance of the pure teachings of Jesus. But the most malicious spite lay hidden behind all of this, and he merely lay in wait for anyone from whom he might entice a rational idea, only to immediately scream "anathema" <term>Anathema: a formal religious curse or excommunication; a Greek word used here to describe a total spiritual condemnation</term> over them and hand them over to the arm of worldly power. Even the pupils, who were to be trained as future teachers of the people—even these he was not ashamed

The good-natured <term>Schwärmer: a religious enthusiast or fanatic; someone whose religious devotion is driven by emotion and imagination rather than reason</term> will never act against morality as soon as his own principles ring clearly in his ears; or, if he does act against them, he will never justify his conduct or present it as a consequence of his obedience to God. <meta>This page continues the psychological profile of Balthasar, specifically focusing on his intellectual shortcomings and his calculated use of religious fundamentalism to further his career. It follows from the previous page's discussion of his lack of academic diligence.</meta>

His <term>Phantasie: imagination or fancy</term> had bypassed his <term>Verstand: understanding or intellect</term> and deluded his mind into accepting its own creations as objective truths. Regarding dogmatic subjects, he was able to reason quite rationally and to distinguish the absurd from the instructive and useful very well; however, he feigned the role of the <term>Hyperorthodoxen: an ultra-traditionalist who adheres strictly to established church doctrine</term> because he considered this the easiest means to make a great name for himself and to secure a sufficient income.

It was for this reason alone that he remained fixed upon the literal word of the <term>Bibel: the Bible</term>, looked down with contempt on all <term>Exegese: the critical interpretation of religious texts</term> and <term>Kritik: textual criticism</term>, and disdained the study of <term>Alterthumskunde: the study of antiquity, including history and classical languages</term>. This was not because he was truly convinced of the futility of these sciences; rather, they seemed to offer him the <term>theologischen Lorbeer: theological laurels; a metaphor for academic or professional honors in divinity</term> at too high a price. He did not wish to be denied the reward, but he rejected the hard work required to earn it, and for that reason alone he cast those disciplines aside.

<summary>This page describes how Balthasar deliberately chose a path of religious fundamentalism not out of conviction, but as a shortcut to wealth and status. He rejected scholarly criticism and historical study because they required more intellectual effort than he was willing to exert.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the psychological and theological critique of Balthasar, moving from his rejection of academic rigor to his idiosyncratic views on morality and piety. It explores his belief that ethics are merely a byproduct of divine authority rather than an internal virtue.</meta>

Not a single idea was to be found in his head that did not have its peculiar side, or one that he could have held onto in order to achieve internal consistency. He considered <term>Sittlichkeit: morality or ethical conduct</term> to be something quite extrinsic—a mere result arising from the relationship of our <term>Knechtschaft: servitude or bondage</term> to the <term>Alleinherrschaft Gottes: the absolute rule or autocracy of God</term>, and something specifically distinct from <term>Frömmigkeit: piety</term>. He was often known to remark quite wittily that <term>Moral: morality</term> belongs to the "Department of Police," <note>In this period, "Police" (Polizey) referred to the general internal administration and regulation of a state's order and public welfare, rather than just criminal law enforcement.</note> and that a servant of religion should concern himself only with piety. 

To him, however, piety meant that behavior which is natural to us when we exist in a state of <term>Kindschaft Gottes: divine childship; the spiritual status of being a child of God</term>. To attain this, he believed nothing more was required than for us to "turn ourselves round and round" and become like little children. <note>A reference to Matthew 18:3: "Unless you turn and become like children, you will never enter the kingdom of heaven."</note> Wherever any intellectual vigor or activity could still be found in him, he applied it to connecting his own idiosyncratic notions with Christian and Jewish mythology. Since he did not want it said of him that he did not also, in passing...

"I do not know," he said, "what <note>Immanuel Kant (1724–1804), the Enlightenment philosopher whose work on human perception was then the height of academic fashion.</note> Kant wants with his <term>Formen der Sinnlichkeit: forms of sensibility</term> and especially with his concept of time, since I have long since cleared all this up; time is nothing other than the relationship of all finite existences—all to one, and one to all." Concerning the outflow of all things from a <term>Urprinzip: primal principle</term>, from the archangel down to the dust, he knew how to speak with such fluency that one would have sworn the Deity had consulted him during the deliberation of the <term>Schöpfungsplan: plan of creation</term>. He was not yet an evil spirit, but he already lacked all those qualities which could only have been granted to him through the union of the Father and Spirit with the Word—especially internal consistency, receptivity to higher effects of grace, and—what must seem strange—faith in the Word. 

From him, this sole product of the <term>Zweyeinigkeit: duality; a satirical play on the word "Trinity" (Dreyeinigkeit), suggesting a lesser, incomplete union of only two divine persons</term>, the Devil then emanated. He developed the principle of evil within himself by entering into a kind of <term>Mißheurath: misalliance or "bad marriage"</term> and mixing himself with a material-spiritual creature subordinate to him—a "schema" of the <note>Followers of the philosopher Immanuel Kant; the author mocks them by linking their abstract terminology to the birth of the Devil.</note> Kantians. 

Through this act, the first evil came into the world, and through it <note>A common name for the Devil or a high-ranking demon.</note> Beelzebub gained the power to act upon the material realm. To check the knave in his career of evil, <note>The personal name of God in the Hebrew Bible.</note> Jehovah banished him to the center of the earth and bound him there with

He called this way of remodeling allegories into tasteless fables "Protestantism," and the blind belief in the phantasms of this rabble "orthodoxy."

To him, the Bible was a book that contained the core of all sciences within itself; it appeared to be a mere shell to the unregenerate <note>original: "Unwiedergebohrnen"; referring to those who have not experienced spiritual "new birth," a central concept in Pietism and mystical Christianity.</note>, but could become everything to the reborn. "Renounce all scientific study," he once addressed a candidate <note>original: "Kandidat"; usually a theology graduate who is a candidate for the ministry.</note>, "for these so-called and highly praised sciences are merely miserable patchwork. Learn to understand Holy Scripture through faith and rebirth, and you will find in it a treasure-trove of all human knowledge." "Even of mathematics?" the candidate replied. "Even of mathematics!" was his answer. "In which verse, then, might I find the Pythagorean theorem <note>The fundamental principle in geometry that the square of the hypotenuse is equal to the sum of the squares of the other two sides.</note> set forth, my highly esteemed Mr. Balthasar?" — "Oh, if you still count that as mathematics, then I pity you from the bottom of my heart!" And with that, the conversation was abruptly cut short.

They therefore sought to attach themselves to **Gutwill**, the son and successor of the great Kollonda. This prince was distinguished by the most excellent qualities of the heart, was anything but ignorant, and had only one single fault: to believe that everyone close to him was as free of deceit as he was himself. —interest in humanity that glows through him; as soon as he is unable to speak from heart to heart, as soon as he is dead to pure morality <term>Sittlichkeit: a key Enlightenment term referring to ethical conduct rooted in inner character rather than just external laws</term>, he will chain himself to theory—the orthodox man to his mysteries, the heterodox man to philosophical platitudes. Both are equally contemptible, for neither fulfills his duty. One must always start from morality, seeking to develop, purify, and strengthen the feeling for it, and place the truth unnoticed in the common man's path so that he might find it as if by himself, rather than seeking to dazzle him with it.

Had Balthasar led those young and partially ignorant hotheads back to this point—had he made morality the foundation of his religious reform <note>original: "Religionsverbesserung"</note>—he would have earned the gratitude of all honest people and proven himself worthy of his prince's favor. But he instead went to war against any and all Enlightenment <note>original: "Aufklärung"</note>, especially against moral enlightenment; he took stupidity under his protection and mocked reason <note>original: "Vernunft"</note>. He now issued an edict which, in the name of the Prince, made it the duty of all preachers and schoolteachers to teach according to the accepted ecclesiastical norm. His edict differed from the Prussian Edict on Religion <term>Prussian Edict on Religion: The *Religionsedikt* of 1788, issued by Johann Christoph von Wöllner, which attempted to curb the influence of Enlightenment rationalism in the church and enforce orthodox doctrine.</term>—which is well-known in our Germany and which I do not wish to stain with my criticism, being convinced of its legitimacy—in that he applied the norm to the interpretation of every biblical passage and wanted it extended even over moral matters. One should no longer, the cited edict states, feed the people with "empty morality," which leaves the heart cold, but rather lead them to a state of being children of God <note>original: "Kindschaft Gottes," a theological term for the relationship between the believer and the Divine.</note> through faith.

A morality where the heart remains cold? — Connect a concept to these words, whoever is able; I cannot!

Hardly had the edict appeared when it outraged everyone; it brought forth pasquinades <note>original: "Pasquille"—anonymous lampoons or satirical leaflets, often posted in public places.</note> and several serious writings in great abundance. The Pridonians <note>This is likely an anagram or pseudonym for "Prussians" (*Preußen*), used by the author to thinly veil a critique of the Prussian state.</note> and the collective Protestant Americans <note>A continuation of the geographical allegory, likely referring to the religious landscape of Northern Germany under the guise of "America."</note> did not fail to recognize the good intentions of the Prince; indeed, the more noble of the people revered them; they were only incensed against the drafter <term>drafter: original German "Koncipient," referring specifically to the author of the legal text, widely identified in history as Johann Christoph von Wöllner.</term>. He was accused of having twisted everything into ambiguities in order to make contradictory interpretations possible and to open the doors wide for the hierarchy. The common man complained that his preacher would now no longer announce what he was personally convinced of, but rather whatever fit the "norm." During the times of the primeval world <note>original: "Urwelt" — referring to the earliest age of the world, often viewed in mystical traditions as a time of direct communion with the divine</note>, God descended to fallen humanity and equipped those among them whom He found worthy of trial with more spirit and power than He had allowed to fall to the lot of the rest of the common mass of humanity. He developed in them the capacity to tear their souls free from the bonds of the body, to <term>disorganize themselves</term> <note>original: "desorganisiren" — in 18th-century mystical and medical contexts, this refers to the suspension of physical sensory functions to allow the soul to act independently of the body</note>, and gifted them with a new sense. Through this sense, in the state of their "disorganization," they were able to behold things outside of themselves in a form other than that of space and time. 

Because of this, they were able to manifest their effectiveness in a twofold way: as physical beings, by means of their bodily strengths under the guidance of their understanding and reason; and as supersensory spirits, through the new sense developed within them, which required no guidance from understanding or reason, but was everything in itself and through itself. Through this double sensitivity, they were citizens of a twofold world—the world of bodies and the world of spirits—and they knew just as much about the pneumatic <note>original: "pneumatisch" — from the Greek 'pneuma' for breath or spirit; refers to spiritual or non-material realities</note> as the physical...

One only needs to know how to pivot skillfully when faced with a refusal, and all is won. Balthasar was a master of this art. "I would have to be the most despicable of men, my most gracious Prince and Lord," he said, "if it were my goal to lead the good people of Pridonia <note>Pridonia likely refers to a fictionalized or pseudonymous state used by the author to discuss contemporary German politics without risk of prosecution.</note> back into ignorance. On the contrary, my wish is to see them in the full radiance of the Light <note>A reference to the Enlightenment (the "Aufklärung"), which used light as its primary symbol.</note>. But is everything truly 'light' that our modern, industrious writers so eagerly try to sell as such? — The French Revolution has stirred a fermentation in human spirits that seems destined to end only with the total dissolution of our established system of ideas. To check this fermentation, restricting the book trade <note>A common euphemism for state censorship of the press and the distribution of radical literature.</note> seemed to me the most effective means. Your Serene Highness <note>original: "Ew. Durchlaucht," a formal address for a reigning prince.</note> does not wish it; very well! It is for you alone to command, and for me to submit to your exalted will. As a loyal subject and a Christian, however, I feel compelled to tell you that your throne stands or falls with the pure doctrine of Christianity."

<summary>In this passage, the advisor Balthasar attempts to justify his calls for censorship by framing the French Revolution as a dangerous "fermentation" of ideas and arguing that the stability of the monarchy is inextricably linked to the protection of religious orthodoxy.</summary>
<keywords>Balthasar, Pridonia, censorship, French Revolution, Enlightenment, book trade, Christian doctrine, political stability</keywords>

**Zellerdal** was a young, amiable man, a man of action and vigor, one of our best pulpit orators <term>Kanzelredner: a preacher noted for their rhetorical skill and ability to move a congregation from the pulpit</term>. The oldest theologians considered it no shame to hold him in high esteem despite his youth, and his congregation adored him. He was a worthy counterpart to the young Ridela, except that his inner fire expressed itself more in gentle meekness and did not burst forth so much in a whirling storm. A pleasant smile usually played about his mouth, and in this smile, one saw not that self-satisfaction which is a sign of dull, comfortable pride, but rather its heavenly sister, which flows from a pure conscience.

Zellerdal was not only a fine orator, a thorough scholar, and an upright man; he was also an amiable husband and a tender father. Nothing lay closer to his heart than the welfare of his congregation and its elevation to a higher state of culture <note>original: "Kultur"; in this context, it refers to the Enlightenment ideal of "Bildung," the moral and intellectual refinement of the citizenry.</note>. And never has any of the greatest orthodox zealots, from Cyril <note>Cyril of Alexandria (c. 376–444 AD), a Church Father known for his intense and often confrontational defense of Christian doctrine.</note> to the German Hunnius <note>Aegidius Hunnius (1550–1603), a prominent Lutheran theologian famous for his rigid defense of Lutheranism against other denominations.</note>, who...

<summary>This page provides a glowing character sketch of Zellerdal, a young and beloved preacher noted for his gentleness, scholarly depth, and commitment to his community's moral improvement.</summary>

<margin>Noesselt at Halle</margin>
**Noesselin** <note>Johann August Nösselt (1734–1807), a prominent German Protestant theologian at Halle known for his moderate, Enlightenment-oriented views.</note>, Professor of Theology at the University of Halle. We can say nothing more concise in his praise than that he was a worthy successor to Luther and a friend of humanity. One will feel even more what it means when it is claimed that someone is a worthy successor of Luther as soon as one casts an eye upon the Jesuits and hears the complaints they break out into against this worthy man.

--- Page 178 ---
<meta>This page continues the satirical comparison between religious reformers and those who oppose the Enlightenment. It picks up the metaphor of the "sheepfold" from page 161, where the author ironically defends the "tearing down" of religious walls as an act of liberation rather than destruction.</meta>

<page-num>162</page-num>

Noesselin had indeed, like his forefather Luther, kindled no wars and given no signal for human slaughters; yet he had honestly contributed his part to that irreconcilable discord. He sought to awaken the voice of reason <note>German: *Vernunft*—the central pillar of Enlightenment thought, emphasizing logic and critical thinking over blind faith</note> in every one of his listeners and readers, to enliven the sense of pure morality, and to work against the belief in the supernatural power of the mystics and the supposed merit of mysticism itself. He, too, worked to tear down and utterly destroy the wooden wall of the well-bolted sheepfold—not to abandon the sheep as prey to the wolves, but to allow them to delight in beautiful nature under the protection of the most vigilant and faithful shepherd, Reason, and to breathe freely under God’s serene heaven. When one day the wolves in sheep’s clothing—both Catholic and non-Catholic <note>original: *akatholische*; here the author refers to reactionary Protestants or "crypto-Jesuits" who mimicked the tactics of the Jesuit order</note> Jesuits—stand fully unmasked, when posterity recognizes them as the scum of humanity and sees in them those individuals who...

Mens <note>Catchword: likely the beginning of *Menschengeschlecht* (human race) or *Menschheit* (humanity)</note>

<summary>The author defends Noesselin (and by extension Luther) against charges of heresy, arguing that breaking down the "walls" of traditional religious dogma allows humanity to live governed by reason rather than mysticism. It concludes with a harsh critique of "Jesuitical" thinkers who disguise themselves to manipulate the public.</summary>
<keywords>Noesselin, Martin Luther, Reason (Vernunft), Morality, Mysticism, Jesuits, Enlightenment, religious freedom</keywords>

human race on its path toward perfection and sought to wickedly degrade it to the level of animals; nevertheless, posterity will bless a man like Noesselin <note>The original text uses the spelling "Röfselin," a variation of the name Noesselin mentioned on the previous page.</note> and offer grateful sacrifices to his memory on the altar of Reason <note>German: *Vernunft*—the guiding principle of the Enlightenment, prioritizing logic and evidence over dogma.</note>.

<margin>Dr. Tebr</margin>
**Tiedelinge**, also a Doctor and Professor of Theology in Hallanda. Exegesis <note>The critical explanation or interpretation of religious texts, specifically the Bible.</note> was his primary field, and for that very reason, he remained a thorn in Balthasar’s side. The latter could not forgive him for leading his audience onto the stage where Jesus had actually lived and worked, nor for acquainting them with the spirit of that historical era. Tiedelinge initiated them into the allegorical <note>A style of speech or writing where symbols represent deeper spiritual or moral meanings.</note> mode of expression used by the peoples of the East and revealed the plan by which the Deity sought to elevate the human race to a higher state of culture through Jesus. Even less forgivable, in Balthasar's view, was that Tiedelinge clearly explained the concept of divine inspiration to his students. By doing so, he cut off all possible side-paths that might lead behind the literal word and the spirit of the Scripture toward some secret meaning understood by only a few <unclear>understandable</unclear>

<meta>This page continues the speech of Balthasar from page 165, where he began questioning the identity and motives of the capital's leading theologians. The text picks up mid-sentence, elaborating on his critique of Enlightenment-era rationalist theology.</meta>

[Who are] they? — Men who have the audacity to claim that the religion of Jesus <note>original: *Religion Jesu*</note> is in harmony with natural religion <note>A theological movement of the Enlightenment that sought a religion based on reason and morality rather than divine revelation or miracles.</note> and merely goes slightly further than it, by providing us with the surest means to secure our happiness through pure conduct, both in this life and for eternity! Men who deny the higher meaning of the Scriptures <note>original: *Schrift*</note>—a meaning granted only to us—and who believe in no mysteries! Their teachings sound beautiful and captivate the common people, but they are merely hollow sounds and possess no substance. Once the people begin to reflect upon their own happiness <note>original: *Glückseligkeit*; often implying both earthly well-being and spiritual salvation.</note>, they must soon realize that everything here on earth is not as it should be. And then, according to the teachings of these gentlemen, it would be the duty of a Christian to transform that which is not as it should be into what it ought to be. Now, suppose the fault lay with the authorities <note>original: *Obrigkeit*; the established ruling power or government.</note>—would the people then, in such a case, have the right to use force against those authorities? In that case, would uprising and rebellion be considered "Christianity" in certain instances, while loyalty to the Prince <note>original: *Fürst*</note> and his government be viewed as the service of the devil and a vice?

No one is perhaps more convinced than I that the educator <note>original: *Erzieher*</note> and the religious teacher are the only secure pillars of the state, but can I act? Do I have the power to act? — If I recommend an honest, simple man <note>original: *einfältigen*; in this context, the word implies a humble, sincere faith rather than a lack of intelligence</note> to be an educator, I know for certain that he will fail to be appointed for that very reason. Our schools are degenerating more and more into revolutionary clubs.

If I wish to remove a preacher from his office because he seems suspicious to me as a Neologist <term>Neolog: a theologian who attempted to modernize Christian doctrine by explaining away miracles and mysteries through reason</term> and a cold moralist, I feel paralyzed in that endeavor as well. The courts demand proof, and I am forced to stand as the accuser against my own subordinates as the defendants. Since the courts always decide according to [the laws]...

…have unjustly belittled me to Your High-Princely Serene Highness and placed my conduct of office in a disadvantageous light before You. Your Serene Highness—the love and delight of your faithful subjects, you, who are in deed and truth the Father of the Fatherland <note>original: "Vater des Vaterlands"; a traditional honorary title for a ruler perceived as a benevolent provider</note>—could otherwise never cast an angry glance upon one of your most faithful subjects. <meta>This page continues the narrative of Balthasar’s political and religious maneuvering. Following the circulating satires mentioned on page 185, Balthasar attempts to defend his religious policies against accusations of subverting the Protestant faith.</meta>

He certainly sought to protect himself against the accusation that he wanted to undermine Protestantism by claiming that he was specifically seeking to maintain pure Lutheranism—the only thing the Prince <note>Referring to the ruler of "Pridonia," likely representing Frederick William II of Prussia.</note> had actually entrusted to him. However, people cried out loudly: anyone who accepts a **symbolic book** <term>symboliſches Buch: A "symbolical book" refers to the formal confessions of faith (like the Augsburg Confession) that define a church's official doctrine. Critics here argue that placing these books on par with the Bible is "un-Protestant."</term> in any form ceases to be a Protestant. Protestantism, they argued, rejects all foreign authorities alongside the **Holy Scriptures** and builds solely and exclusively upon them.

Luther says: “Secular authority, princes, lords, and lawyers can make laws, provide rights and teachings regarding house, yard, villages, land, people, and everything on earth that is subject to man. But to want to act and behave in matters of faith and concerning the soul as one does with external and physical things—with oxen, with house and yard!—**that is not to be tolerated!**” 

He himself <note>Balthasar</note> admits, “**God alone has the right to command faith**”; now, if God alone has the right to command faith, how then could he presume to take this right for himself— <note>The sentence breaks off here, continuing onto the next page. <meta>This page continues the discussion of Balthasar's realization that simply controlling a secret society is not enough to control the minds of its members. ...attends a Lodge, is thereby blindly devoted to its superior, no more than someone who adheres to the Lutheran Church swears by every word that Luther once said. Balthasar therefore clearly saw that it was no easy undertaking to convince all those he had admitted of the truth of his claims, much less to instill his spirit into them. But what was gained through the Order without this? — Nothing! If anything was to be gained, his spirit of persecution <note>original: "Verfolgungsgeist"</note> had to animate all its members, and his fanaticism <term>Schwärmerey: a pejorative Enlightenment-era term for excessive religious enthusiasm, mystical zeal, or "enthusiasm" that bypasses reason</term> had to have infected them all.

He further believed that the Pridonian scribomania <note>original: "Schreibseligkeit"; a pejorative term for the 18th-century explosion of pamphlets and journals</note> seldom allowed a significant event in the political or literary world to pass by without debating the pros and cons—but mostly just the "over"—in ephemeral pamphlets <note>original: "fliegenden und bald wieder verfliegenden Blättern," literally "flying and soon-flying-away leaves"</note> with a more generous expenditure of paper than of intellectual power; in the future, one should avoid all pros, cons, and "overs" in theological matters, and instead leave everything nicely as it was of old. The theological faculty in <margin>Halle</margin> Hallanda should set the example by suppressing this mania for writing and seeking to make it hateful to their students; in return, however, they could expect that the Commission of Faith—or more correctly, the Wardship College in Divine Matters <note>original Latin: "Puppillenkollegium in divinis"; a "Pupillenkollegium" was a court for orphans/wards, here sarcastically suggesting the church treats the public as children</note>—would assure them of its grateful grace, just as Franz <note>Likely a reference to Emperor Francis I or II</note> assured Wiener Neustadt upon the delivery of the 3000 guilders for the costs of war. The following day, the holy Balthasar <note>The narrator uses "holy" ironically here, referring to Balthasar's high standing in the hypocritical secret order described on the previous page.</note> set out on his journey. Scarcely had he left Belinda when a dark foreboding already seemed to warn him and advise him to abandon the trip. A Jew <note>In the literature of this period, Jewish characters were frequently used as plot devices involving trade or trickery; here, he serves to initiate a comedic complication for the protagonist.</note>, who intended to bring in a package of Brabant lace <note>original: "Brabanter Spitzen." He saw no other way to save himself than by handing the package over to our saint, claiming it was a gift sent by a nearby village preacher which he wished to deliver now, having just happened to meet him. It was a stroke of luck for the man that while he knew Balthasar, he himself was entirely unknown to Balthasar. Balthasar accepted the gift and placed it in his carriage box <note>original: "Kutschkasten"—the storage compartment built into a horse-drawn carriage.</note>. Scarcely was this business concluded when the man immediately set off again at a full gallop, then gradually slowed to a trot, and finally to a walk.

—dear fellow clergyman <term>Amtsbruder</term>, I do not wish to [argue] with you, but enough—theology must be overthrown, even if only for the sake of the Prince and the State." — I would certainly be curious to learn to what extent the Princes and Neology <term>Neologie</term> <note>A 18th-century movement in German theology that used reason to adapt Christian doctrine to Enlightenment values, often seen as a threat by traditionalists.</note> behave like opposites <term>Antipoden</term> <note>Literally "antipodes," meaning things that are diametrically opposed, like the north and south poles.</note> to one another? —

"That is easy to see. Neology brings about the Enlightenment <term>Aufklärung</term>, and this must restrict the Princes more and more from day to day, in the same degree to which it approaches its perfection. It eventually transforms the Prince from a sovereign <term>Souverain</term> into the first servant of the state <note>A famous phrase associated with Frederick the Great of Prussia, suggesting the monarch's duty is to the welfare of the state rather than personal whim.</note> and cuts off all his self-will." —

And must he then have the latter? — Before that expression, however, you surely meant to say "into a Father of the Fatherland" <note>original: "Vater des Vaterlands." A traditional title (Latin: *Pater Patriae*) used to describe a ruler who cares for his subjects as a father cares for his children.</note>. —

"But into a father to whom the children refuse all obedience as soon as he appears to rule unjustly." —

Not *appears*, but *actually* rules. But surely you would not want to claim to all the world that any regent <term>Regent</term> strives to want to rule unjustly. This would be to slan— <note>The word is cut off at the end of the page; the catchword indicates the full word is "verläumden" (to slander).</note>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue regarding the political effects of the Enlightenment. The previous page ended mid-word with "con-" (o-), leading into the word "conspiracies" (Verschwörungen) at the start of this page.</meta>

...conspiracies spun in the deepest darkness, and brought to light only just before their complete outbreak. Is there perhaps any land, taken as a whole, more enlightened <term>aufgeklärter</term> than the Prussian, Brunswick, Gotha, and Weimar <note>These territories were famous centers of the German Enlightenment (Aufklärung) and were known for their relatively progressive rulers and intellectual culture.</note> territories? And are kings and princes any more secure anywhere than precisely in these lands? They are honored; they are worshipped there. So please, explain this strange riddle to me: how can the Enlightenment <term>Aufklärung</term> <note>The 18th-century intellectual movement emphasizing reason and individualism over tradition.</note> be harmful to the prince, when princes live nowhere more safely than in the most enlightened lands? Who has resisted the French as much as the Prussians? And who is less of a "praying brother" <term>Betbruder</term> <note>A derogatory term for someone who is excessively or hypocritically pious; the author uses it here to suggest that Prussian soldiers fight out of civic duty and reason rather than religious fervor.</note> than the officer and soldier of this army? He allows his two first-born sons to be joined, according to their own inclination, with two of the most amiable princesses; the people recognize the benefit bestowed upon them thereby; they see that parents who love one another [will provide] its future...

<summary>The speaker argues that Enlightenment principles do not lead to revolution, but rather strengthen the bond between a monarch and his subjects, citing the stability and loyalty of the Prussian state as primary evidence.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the speaker's defense of the Enlightenment as a stabilizing force for monarchy. It follows the previous page's discussion of Frederick William's popularity and the marriage of his sons.</meta>

<image-desc>A decorative horizontal printer's ornament consisting of symmetrical scrolls and flourishes.</image-desc>

...st worthy heirs to the throne, and rejoices in the most joyful ecstasy.

The solemn union takes place; the King desires that his people share in it. They gather by the thousands at his palace, and he—together with his noble family—can read nothing but reverence and joy in the gaze of these thousands surrounding him. In Berlin, it is unheard of to sense even the slightest rebellion even in the dimmest distance. Will it be any different in our Prisdonia? <note>Prisdonia (Prisdonien) appears to be a fictional or pseudonymized name for a state used within this dialogue to represent a typical German principality.</note> And does the Enlightenment <term>Aufklärung</term> <note>The 18th-century intellectual movement emphasizing reason and individualism over tradition.</note> still seem so hateful to you, as an honest friend of the prince? 

"But did not this very King issue a Religious Edict?" <note>This refers to the Woellner Edict of 1788, a controversial Prussian law that attempted to curb the influence of the Enlightenment on religious teaching.</note> — which, let it be well understood, he himself called merely an ecclesiastical police edict <term>Polizeyedikt</term> <note>In the 18th century, "police" (Polizei) referred to the general internal administration and maintenance of public order within a state.</note>. Its purpose was more to restrain the religious agitators <term>Religionsstürmer</term> in their wicked assaults than to impose an unheard-of coercion of conscience on enlightened Protestants, or to hinder the free investigation of theological matters from the university lecterns. found themselves branded with contempt. It became part of the proper **student spirit** <term>Burschenton</term> <note>The "Burschenton" refers to the specific code of conduct, etiquette, and social tone adopted by university students (Burschen) in the 18th and 19th centuries.</note> to be mindful of one’s purpose and to remain true to it. Unrestrained behavior was no longer seen as the most precious treasure of **academic freedom**, but rather, students began to find that freedom more in casting off the chains of burdensome social etiquette than in the total denial of all manners. The student recognized that he had to accept certain restrictions for the sake of others, just as others had to accept restrictions for his sake; he realized that he had duties as well as rights. He had ceased to view himself as the despot over the **Philistines** <term>Philister</term> <note>A derogatory term used by German students to describe non-students or townspeople, implying they were materialistic and lacked culture.</note>, without thereby sinking to the level of being a slave to the **professors**. He had become a good man and a good citizen who, while possessing a full sense and knowledge of his rights, held an equally deep sense and correct knowledge of his duties. In this state of affairs, the "Brother Catechumens" <note>In the context of secret societies, "Catechumens" were the lowest rank of initiates or novices undergoing instruction before full admission.</note> were unable to exert any influence despite their best intentions; the **Orders** <term>Orden</term> <note>Secret student societies, often modeled after Freemasonry, which were frequently banned by university authorities for their secrecy and involvement in duels.</note> to which they belonged lay crushed and defeated in the dust.

<summary>This page describes a shift in 18th-century German student culture away from rowdy, "despotic" behavior toward a more civic-minded sense of duty and academic purpose. This cultural change led to the decline of secret student orders and the "Catechumens" who sought to recruit for them.</summary>

Meanwhile, several **clubs** <term>Klubbs</term> <note>In the late 18th century, these "clubs" represented a shift away from the secret, often violent student "Orders" toward more open associations focused on sociability, literature, and moral improvement.</note> had formed among the young people, who made it a law for themselves to enjoy pleasure with the proper subordination to duty—moderately, and as the spice of life—and to maintain a tone in their enjoyment that would not be offensive even to a wise man. These clubs were held in general high regard and were loved by everyone. Philosophers considered it an honor to be admitted to them, and the most respectable professors of theology were delighted to live in their midst, like fathers in a circle of hopeful sons.

In one of these clubs, the news was first told that the "holy" **Balthasar** <note>This is likely a satirical nickname for a specific historical figure or a recognizable archetype of a pedantic, overly-strict academic reformer.</note> was on his way to **Hallanda** <note>A pseudonym often used in academic novels of this period to refer to the University of Halle.</note> and intended to give birth there to those great **reforms** with which his **clogged brain** <note>Original German: *verschleimtes Gehirn*. In the medical language of the time, a "phlegm-filled" brain suggested someone who was intellectually sluggish, dull, or stuck in outdated modes of thinking.</note> was pregnant. The attention of the club members was most vividly stimulated by this news. Each of them saw these highly-praised reforms not only as encroachments on the rights of **academic freedom** <term>akademische Freyheit</term> <note>The traditional right of German students to manage their own lives and studies without strict supervision, a privilege they guarded fiercely against administrative interference.</note>, but even as encroachments on the

**Balthasar.** Should I really be welcome here?

**Student.** Why do you ask, dear uncle <note>original: *Onkle*; an older spelling of *Onkel* (uncle).</note>, as if you doubted it? And yet this question comes so hastily that I might believe you are more concerned with its answer than with embracing your nephew?

**Balthasar.** Forgive me, good lad, you are indeed most welcome to me! (**he embraces him.**) — So, I truly need not doubt that I am welcome to the students here?

**Student.** He who is conscious of pure and good intentions must never doubt!

**Balthasar.** But whether the purity and goodness of my intentions are also appreciated: That, dear nephew, that is the question?

<summary>Balthasar embraces his nephew, a student at the university, while expressing anxiety over whether his religious or reformist intentions will be well-received by the student body.</summary>

**Student.** Surely you don't hold such a poor opinion of the moral character of the students here as to think they wouldn't appreciate the purity and goodness of your intentions?

**Balthasar.** You are evading my question; but I will speak more plainly—will they bring me a cheer <term>Vivat: A traditional Latin shout meaning "Long live!" often used by German students as a formal public tribute or serenade to honor a professor or guest.</term>?

**Student.** It is nearly ten o'clock and already quite dark—yes! This would be exactly the time they would have to bring it to you!

**Balthasar.** You won't come out with it, but I see clearly that you want me to be surprised, and I wouldn't want to spoil your fun.

**Student.** Yes! Dear uncle <note>original: *Onkle*; an archaic spelling of *Onkel*</note>, you truly will be surprised!

**Balthasar.** Will I? Oh! That is wonderful! That is excellent! (*He goes to the window.*) Surely I hear—

<summary>Balthasar eagerly anticipates a traditional student serenade in his honor, misinterpreting his nephew's ambiguous warnings about an upcoming "surprise" as a sign of respect.</summary>

—what a massive din outside! (he opens the window) yes, all the students are already gathered; it will probably start any moment now. Johann? A cup of tea. I will just drink tea, so that I may be found completely unprepared. (The tumult outside the window grew louder; the students approached in throngs, and suddenly the following words were heard:

> Down with <note>original: *Pereat!*; a Latin expression meaning "may he perish," used by German students as a formal public shaming or protest—the direct opposite of a *Vivat*.</note> the clique of obscurantists <note>original: *Verfinsterungsklique*; literally "darkening-clique," a derogatory term for those perceived as opposing the Enlightenment or intellectual progress.</note>! ### Balthasar.
My God, what is this?
(He turns pale and foams with rage and annoyance.)

### Student.
Truly not a cheer <term>Vivat: A traditional Latin shout meaning "Long live!"</term>!—But at least something that seems to surprise you even more than that.

Scarcely had the noise died down when stones and buckshot flew in through the window and the panes clattered.

### A Preacher’s Widow.
Save yourself, Your Reverence!

--- Page 254 ---
<meta>This page continues from page 235, where Balthasar was confronted by a riotous student protest (a "Pereat") outside his window. Having just been told to save himself by a preacher's widow as stones began to fly, he now attempts to maintain a facade of religious stoicism.</meta>

**Balthasar.** (*The cup of tea in his hand.*) No! I am everywhere in God's hands, and thus I will not flee from Him. (*A stone smashes his cup—he flees.*) The end of my faith! <note>original: *Finis fidei!*; Balthasar ironically abandons his religious confidence the moment his physical safety is threatened.</note>

Scarcely had the morning dawned—to speak in the language of everyday life—when our "Saint" immediately left Hallanda with the firm resolution never to set his holy feet upon this unholy place again. The University, which meanwhile could assume that he would not let this insult go unavenged, did everything in its power to identify the instigators of this youthful mischief; at the same time, they informed the Prince of the incident via a courier <note>original: *Stafette*; an express relay messenger used for urgent communications.</note> sent to their curator in Belinda. The former effort was in vain; not a single one of the instigators could be tracked down. 

The students even went so far as to auction off the tree-cake <term>Baumkuchen: A traditional German "king of cakes" baked on a spit; its layers resemble the rings of a tree. Here, it likely refers to a cake intended for the celebration Balthasar expected.</term> in the "Golden Hyena" <note>likely the name of a local tavern or inn.</note> and carried the purchased pieces around as if they were "orthodox relics." So little contrition did they feel for their deed.

<summary>Balthasar quickly abandons his pious stoicism when a stone smashes his tea cup, fleeing the town of Hallanda in disgrace while the local students mockingly auction off his celebratory cake as "relics."</summary>
