<meta>This page continues the aftermath of the student riot in Hallanda. While the previous page described the students' mockery of Balthasar, this page shifts to the political consequences and the Prince’s reaction to Balthasar's religious interference.</meta>

...and so little were they inclined to betray the instigators of the riot. Even preachers are said to have brought them stones and buckshot <note>original: *Schrot*; small lead pellets typically used for hunting, here used as projectiles during the unrest.</note>. The Prince, however, took it very ungraciously that Balthasar had meddled in something for which he had given him no mandate. He began to realize that Balthasar was a fanatic <term>schwärme: from *Schwärmerei*, a term used by Enlightenment thinkers to describe religious zealotry, irrational mysticism, or "enthusiasm" in a negative sense.</term>, allowed himself to be led in his order by disguised Jesuits, and worked solely toward destroying the progress of human reason—the work of many centuries. Balthasar was therefore immediately placed on inactive status, the order was abolished, and the Enlightenment <term>Aufklärung: the 18th-century intellectual movement that championed reason, science, and individual rights over tradition and religious dogma.</term> was given free rein. Whether this will make Pridonia's <note>Pridonia is the fictional or allegorical setting of the narrative.</note> fortune? — Do you still ask, dear reader! 

Barbaroux <note>Charles Jean Marie Barbaroux (1767–1794), a notable French revolutionary and Girondist deputy of the National Convention, famous for his oratorical skills and his eventual execution during the Reign of Terror.</note>, when taking his leave at the club in Marseille to take up his post in Paris as a deputy of the National Convention, displayed a dagger and assured everyone that he possessed nothing more in the world than this dagger. With a similar assurance, the "Saint" Balthasar stepped off the stage. When, by order of the Prince, he forever closed the lodge of the Knights of Faith <term>Glaubensritter: likely a fictionalized secret society or religious order within the story, representing reactionary or anti-Enlightenment interests.</term>—

## Postscript from the Typesetter.

Who might this Saint Balthasar be? And does a "Pridonia" really exist in America? — Honestly speaking, Saint Balthasar exists as little in reality <note>original: *rerum natura*; a Latin phrase meaning "in the nature of things" or "in the physical world."</note> as Pridonia does, but men who think as he does exist in abundance, and there are likewise countries where the Jesuits <note>The Society of Jesus, a Catholic religious order that was a frequent target of Enlightenment-era writers who viewed them as agents of superstition and political interference.</note> would like to play their part. One should therefore not focus on the fiction of individual characters, but rather view this whole work as an instructive fable—as a tale that demonstrates how far fanaticism <term>Schwärmerey: a specific 18th-century term for religious "enthusiasm" or zealotry that Enlightenment thinkers believed bypassed rational thought in favor of dangerous mysticism.</term> can lead a person and how dangerous any religious order can become to a country if it seeks to obstruct all use of reason. Thank God! Germany has no Balthasar to show yet, but several would emerge from the darkness—

<summary>This page concludes the story of Balthasar and transitions into a postscript explaining that the narrative is an allegory meant to warn against the dangers of religious fanaticism and the suppression of reason.</summary>
<keywords>Enlightenment, Jesuits, fanaticism, allegory, reason, Balthasar, Pridonia, religious orders</keywords>

--- Page 257 ---
<meta>This page continues the postscript from page 238. The previous page ended with the phrase "several [Balthasars] would emerge from the darkness—," where the catchword "aus" completes the verb "ausgekrochen" (crawled out) at the start of this page.</meta>

...crawled out, had Frederick William the Much-Beloved <note>Likely King Frederick William III of Prussia (reigned 1797–1840), known for his role in the Napoleonic Wars and the subsequent restoration of peace in Europe.</note> not granted peace to our fatherland and perhaps even internal rest and stability to France itself. Hail to him! To the peace-bringer; hail and obedience to all regents who think and act as he does! The bone of contention <note>original: *Zankapfel*; literally the "apple of discord," a reference to the mythological object that sparked the Trojan War.</note> has been removed, and—all the plans of the Jesuits are hereby destroyed forever. Moreover, it would please the author if a real-life original were to come forward to match his ideal character; this would demonstrate, however improbable it may be, that he has faithfully copied human nature in its aberrations, and that his concerns were not mere chimeras <term>Chimären: an 18th-century term for empty fantasies or unfounded fears, named after the mythical monster made of disparate parts.</term>.

Furthermore, he solemnly and sacredly assures that he did not intend to present anyone personally or depict them based on their individual traits; he merely endeavored to vividly portray the spirit of mysticism, which some would now so gladly graft onto the Protestant Church, and he depicted it within an order which Jesuits had actually planned,

<summary>The author concludes the postscript by praising the peace brought by the Prussian King and clarifies that his story is a generalized critique of religious mysticism rather than an attack on specific individuals.</summary>
<keywords>Frederick William III, Jesuits, mysticism, Protestant Church, allegory, religious fanaticism, peace of Europe</keywords>

<meta>This page completes the sentence from the previous page's postscript. The catchword "aus" joins with the previous "ausgekrochen" to form "ausgekrochen" (crawled out).</meta>

<image-desc>A decorative horizontal printer's ornament consisting of a central floral/scroll motif with extending lines.</image-desc>

...out. Since all this was still not enough to represent a complete picture <note>original: *Gemählde*; used here to mean a literary portrait or vivid depiction.</note>, he imagined a land for himself which—in order to avoid causing offense or providing material for [malicious] interpretations—he relocated to America. In this land, he set to work a man who was entirely a mystic and hyper-orthodox <term>Hyperorthodox: a term for someone who adheres with extreme or excessive zeal to traditional religious dogmas, often used by Enlightenment-era writers to criticize religious hardliners.</term>. This man cleared away everything around him and made plans just as his superiors, the Jesuits, would have him clear away and plan in the real world <note>original: *in rerum natura*; a Latin phrase meaning "in the nature of things" or "in reality."</note>. However, he met the kind of end that the passion for obscurantism <term>Verfinsterungssucht: literally an "addiction to darkening"; a term for the deliberate suppression of Enlightenment knowledge and the promotion of ignorance or superstition.</term> must always meet—he departed the stage, mocked by the youth and both pitied and despised by the noble-minded.
